# Theme 2: How LPs Evaluate Emerging Managers

## Executive Summary

The evaluation of emerging venture capital managers by Limited Partners (LPs) is a nuanced process that balances qualitative human elements with rigorous quantitative analysis. Drawing on insights from leading fund of funds, family offices, and seasoned GPs, this analysis explores the critical criteria LPs use to evaluate and back emerging managers. The overarching theme is that while the current fundraising environment is challenging, emerging managers who possess a clear "why," align their fund structure with their strategy, and cultivate authentic communities are highly sought after for their outperformance potential.

## 1. The "Why" and Manager Archetypes

To cut through the "sea of sameness," LPs prioritize managers who have a compelling and distinctive value proposition. 

- **A Clear Point of View:** Emerging managers must answer the critical question: "Why should this fund exist, and why me?" LPs look for a unique pitch, whether that means identifying opportunities in overlooked sectors, bringing a novel approach to the market, or possessing a specialized thesis (Mar Hershenson, Courtney McCrae).
- **Evaluating by Archetype:** Some LPs, such as Spring Point Partners, use specific "archetypes" to evaluate managers. They look at past experience—such as whether the manager was a former founder, an operator, or a spin-out from an established firm—and benchmark them against historical success rates (Sabrina Bbridge). 
- **Spin-outs vs. Operators:** Spin-outs offer LPs a degree of comfort due to their institutional familiarity and track record. However, operators-turned-investors are deeply compelling when their specific operational background closely matches their investment thesis, allowing them to clearly articulate their value-add to founders (Laura Thompson).

## 2. Portfolio Construction and the "Math" of Small Funds

A critical component of LP due diligence is ensuring that the manager's fund structure and portfolio construction align with their stated strategy.

- **Ownership vs. Fund Size:** Small funds hold a significant mathematical advantage. An emerging manager does not necessarily need a multi-billion dollar exit to generate excellent returns. For example, a $50M fund that secures 10% ownership in a company that exits at $500M can return the entire fund (a "fund returner"). This raises the floor on performance and makes small, concentrated funds highly attractive to LPs looking for alpha (Alex Edelson).
- **Matching Size to Strategy:** LPs analyze whether the fund size matches the manager's approach. An angel or pre-seed strategy works well with a smaller fund backed by high-net-worth individuals and family offices, whereas a Series A strategy requires larger institutional backing (Laura Thompson).
- **Institutional Readiness:** Despite being "emerging," LPs expect a high level of operational professionalism. This includes having audited financials, a quality back-office, standard LPAs, and an organized, comprehensive data room to build trust (Mar Hershenson, Jamie from Screen Door).

## 3. Qualitative Factors: Authenticity, Community, and Resilience

Because early-stage venture is fundamentally a people business, LPs place heavy emphasis on a manager's soft skills and character.

- **Authenticity Over Hype:** LPs are adept at seeing through flash and hype. They evaluate whether a manager is a genuine thought leader or just an "influencer." Authentic community building—whether through large events or intimate 16-person Jeffersonian dinners—must align with the GP's personality (Hari Joy).
- **Community as an Indicator:** How a GP builds their community often serves as a proxy for how they will build their firm and support their portfolio companies. LPs look at the manager's support network to gauge resilience: do they have the people around them to survive the high-stress, lonely moments of firm-building? (Sabrina Bbridge, Constance Thurman).
- **Endurance and Obsession:** LPs look for managers with an "unlimited self-belief," a chip on their shoulder, and an obsession with the problems they are solving. The best emerging managers have endured rejection and possess the grit necessary to survive a 10-14 year LP commitment (Mar Hershenson).

## 4. Navigating the Fundraising Environment

The macroeconomic climate has fundamentally altered how LPs deploy capital to emerging managers.

- **The Denominator Effect:** Following the public market downturns in 2022, many institutional LPs found themselves overallocated to venture capital and were forced to pull back from making new commitments. As a result, fundraising has become significantly more difficult and time-consuming (Mar Hershenson).
- **Targeting the Right LPs:** A common piece of advice for Fund I and Fund II managers is to avoid wasting time on massive institutional pensions prematurely. Instead, emerging managers should target LPs appropriate for their fund size: high-net-worth individuals, family offices, mission-aligned foundations, corporate venture capital (CVC), and state-level SSBCI programs (Courtney McCrae).
- **The Diversity Advantage:** Diverse emerging managers often have access to unique deal flow and networks that traditional funds overlook. While it historically takes diverse managers longer to raise funds, dedicated LP programs (like Recast Capital's Accelerate) are actively working to bridge this gap, driven by data showing that diverse GPs tend to back more diverse and successful founders (Courtney McCrae).

## Conclusion

LPs evaluate emerging managers through a dual lens of quantitative rigor and qualitative intuition. While a tight portfolio model and appropriate fund sizing are prerequisites, the ultimate decision often rests on the manager's authenticity, their ability to articulate a differentiated "why," and the strength of the community they have built around them. In a challenging capital environment, LPs are heavily prioritizing managers who demonstrate the grit, clarity, and institutional readiness necessary to become the enduring franchise firms of tomorrow.
